    "newString": "Classification supervision and MLM thus serve complementary roles: the former stabilizes within the training distribution, the latter generalizes across distributions.",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — D034 analysis reframe\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nOne concern is whether a topic-level shortcut (jailbreak conversations discuss different topics than benign ones) could explain the detection results. Three observations argue against this. First, topic classification on the same jailbreak training data yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD), with one seed falling below vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312); if a topic shortcut drove detection, topic-supervised models should outperform, not underperform, unsupervised alternatives. Second, scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp across all OOD evaluations), indicating that specific label content does not drive detection. Third, MLM without any classification labels achieves strong detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$ on DD), confirming that the benefit comes from domain exposure rather than any classification-based shortcut.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B, raising the concern that the evidence hierarchy reflects within-LLM stylistic transfer rather than beyond-lexical generalization. Evaluation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks from LMSYS) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming that DAPT on synthetic text transfers to human-written attacks. ToxicChat is single-turn, so the relative ordering among domain-adapted variants reflects single-turn modality rather than variant quality. This evidence validates the core DAPT benefit but does not test the full multi-turn pipeline; multi-turn human jailbreak datasets are needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD evaluations, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ relative to MLM. On ToxicChat (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), this pattern reverses: 9-class exhibits high variance (std\\,=\\,0.087) while JB-MLM exhibits the lowest among domain-adapted variants (std\\,=\\,0.013). Classification supervision optimizes representations for the training distribution's structure, stabilizing predictions within the synthetic data manifold; when the test distribution shifts to human-written text, these distribution-specific representations become a source of instability. MLM's unsupervised objective imposes no distribution-specific classification boundaries, producing representations that generalize more uniformly across the style gap. Classification supervision and MLM thus serve complementary roles: the former as a within-distribution regularizer, the latter as a cross-distribution generalizer.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nThe cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur controlled ablation across two encoder architectures and four attack families identifies three findings about cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection. First, domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\\to$ 0.466). Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability on synthetic OOD evaluations (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$ vs.\\ MLM-only) and interpretable strategy characterization regardless of label correctness, though strategy predictions do not transfer to unseen attacks ($\\kappa$=0.126). On human data, this stability pattern reverses: MLM exhibits the lowest variance while classification-supervised variants show the highest, suggesting that classification regularization is distribution-specific (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers beyond synthetic data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}); full multi-turn validation on human datasets is needed.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and OOD evaluation covers three attack families (FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack). This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. Evaluation on ToxicChat (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}) confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written jailbreaks, partially addressing the single-LLM confound. However, ToxicChat contains only single-turn data; whether the full multi-turn evidence hierarchy (including classification supervision's stabilization benefit) generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures discourse features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-seed variants.}\nMean pooling results report a single-seed (seed 42) evaluation. Multi-seed validation is needed to confirm this finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability on synthetic OOD evaluations (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$ vs.\\ MLM-only) and interpretable strategy characterization regardless of label correctness, though strategy predictions do not transfer to unseen attacks ($\\kappa$=0.126). On human data, this stability pattern reverses: MLM exhibits the lowest variance while classification-supervised variants show the highest, suggesting that classification regularization is distribution-specific (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers beyond synthetic data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}); full multi-turn validation on human datasets is needed.",
          "+Our controlled ablation across two encoder architectures on synthetic multi-turn data spanning three OOD attack families identifies three findings. First, domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\\to$ 0.466), though capacity differences (184M vs.\\ 125M) cannot be fully disentangled from pretraining method. Third, classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic data distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$ vs.\\ MLM-only on synthetic OOD), regardless of label correctness; however, this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers beyond synthetic data; multi-turn generalization across generation sources remains to be verified.",

This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. Evaluation on ToxicChat (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}) confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written jailbreaks, partially addressing the single-LLM confound. However, ToxicChat contains only single-turn data; whether the full multi-turn evidence hierarchy (including classification supervision's stabilization benefit) generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks remains open.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — D034 analysis reframe\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nOne concern is whether a topic-level shortcut (jailbreak conversations discuss different topics than benign ones) could explain the detection results. Three observations argue against this. First, topic classification on the same jailbreak training data yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD), with one seed falling below vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312); if a topic shortcut drove detection, topic-supervised models should outperform, not underperform, unsupervised alternatives. Second, scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp across all OOD evaluations), indicating that specific label content does not drive detection. Third, MLM without any classification labels achieves strong detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$ on DD), confirming that the benefit comes from domain exposure rather than any classification-based shortcut.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B, raising the concern that the evidence hierarchy reflects within-LLM stylistic transfer rather than beyond-lexical generalization. Evaluation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks from LMSYS) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming that DAPT on synthetic text transfers to human-written attacks. ToxicChat is single-turn, so the relative ordering among domain-adapted variants reflects single-turn modality rather than variant quality. This evidence validates the core DAPT benefit but does not test the full multi-turn pipeline; multi-turn human jailbreak datasets are needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD evaluations, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ relative to MLM. On ToxicChat (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), this pattern reverses: 9-class exhibits high variance (std\\,=\\,0.087) while JB-MLM exhibits the lowest among domain-adapted variants (std\\,=\\,0.013). Classification supervision optimizes representations for the training distribution's structure, stabilizing predictions within the synthetic data manifold; when the test distribution shifts to human-written text, these distribution-specific representations become a source of instability. MLM's unsupervised objective imposes no distribution-specific classification boundaries, producing representations that generalize more uniformly across the style gap. Classification supervision and MLM thus serve complementary roles: the former stabilizes within the training distribution, the latter generalizes across distributions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nThe cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur controlled ablation across two encoder architectures on synthetic multi-turn data spanning three OOD attack families identifies three findings. First, domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\\to$ 0.466), though capacity differences (184M vs.\\ 125M) cannot be fully disentangled from pretraining method. Third, classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic data distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$ vs.\\ MLM-only on synthetic OOD), regardless of label correctness; however, this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers beyond synthetic data; multi-turn generalization across generation sources remains to be verified.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and OOD evaluation covers three attack families (FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack). This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. Evaluation on ToxicChat (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}) confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written jailbreaks, partially addressing the single-LLM confound. However, ToxicChat contains only single-turn data; whether the full multi-turn evidence hierarchy (including classification supervision's stabilization benefit) generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks remains open.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures discourse features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-seed variants.}\nMean pooling results report a single-seed (seed 42) evaluation. Multi-seed validation is needed to confirm this finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — D034 analysis reframe\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nOne concern is whether a topic-level shortcut (jailbreak conversations discuss different topics than benign ones) could explain the detection results. Three observations argue against this. First, topic classification on the same jailbreak training data yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD), with one seed falling below vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312); if a topic shortcut drove detection, topic-supervised models should outperform, not underperform, unsupervised alternatives. Second, scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp across all OOD evaluations), indicating that specific label content does not drive detection. Third, MLM without any classification labels achieves strong detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$ on DD), confirming that the benefit comes from domain exposure rather than any classification-based shortcut.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B, raising the concern that the evidence hierarchy reflects within-LLM stylistic transfer rather than beyond-lexical generalization. Evaluation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks from LMSYS) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming that DAPT on synthetic text transfers to human-written attacks. ToxicChat is single-turn, so the relative ordering among domain-adapted variants reflects single-turn modality rather than variant quality. This evidence validates the core DAPT benefit but does not test the full multi-turn pipeline; multi-turn human jailbreak datasets are needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nOn synthetic OOD evaluations, classification supervision reduces cross-seed variance 4--20$\\times$ relative to MLM. On ToxicChat (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), this pattern reverses: 9-class exhibits high variance (std\\,=\\,0.087) while JB-MLM exhibits the lowest among domain-adapted variants (std\\,=\\,0.013). Classification supervision optimizes representations for the training distribution's structure, stabilizing predictions within the synthetic data manifold; when the test distribution shifts to human-written text, these distribution-specific representations become a source of instability. MLM's unsupervised objective imposes no distribution-specific classification boundaries, producing representations that generalize more uniformly across the style gap. Classification supervision and MLM thus serve complementary roles: the former stabilizes within the training distribution, the latter generalizes across distributions.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nThe cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur controlled ablation across two encoder architectures on synthetic multi-turn data spanning three OOD attack families identifies three findings. First, domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\\to$ 0.466), though capacity differences (184M vs.\\ 125M) cannot be fully disentangled from pretraining method. Third, classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic data distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$ vs.\\ MLM-only on synthetic OOD), regardless of label correctness; however, this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\\ vanilla gap transfers beyond synthetic data; multi-turn generalization across generation sources remains to be verified.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures discourse features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-seed variants.}\nMean pooling results report a single-seed (seed 42) evaluation. Multi-seed validation is needed to confirm this finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",

    "oldString": "DisPeD's vanilla baseline represents the class of pretrained-encoder-plus-RNN approaches \\emph{without} domain adaptation; it is not a direct proxy for DeepContext, which differs in encoder fine-tuning, model capacity, and loss function. Our contribution is the systematic decomposition showing that domain exposure, not architectural choices, is the primary factor for cross-attack generalization.",
    "newString": "The vanilla baseline in our ablation represents the class of pretrained-encoder-plus-RNN approaches without domain adaptation. Our contribution is the systematic decomposition showing that domain exposure, not architectural choices, is the primary factor for cross-attack generalization.",
    "originalFile": "% related_work.tex — D024 compressed\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}\nMulti-turn attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} implements gradual escalation, FITD~\\citep{fitd2024} exploits commitment-consistency bias, Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} combines emotional manipulation with role assignment, ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering, PAIR~\\citep{chao2023pair} and TAP~\\citep{mehrotra2024tap} automate iterative refinement. \\citet{wei2023jailbroken} provide a unifying taxonomy grounded in competing objectives and mismatched generalization. Standardized evaluation frameworks such as HarmBench~\\citep{mazeika2024harmbench} and JailbreakBench~\\citep{chao2024jailbreakbench} have enabled systematic comparison across attack types. Across these families, a shared reliance on persuasion dynamics~\\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj} distinguishes them from token-level attacks such as GCG~\\citep{zou2023gcg}.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses.}\nExisting defenses model conversation-level structure but do not decompose what enables cross-attack generalization. DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU; however, its evaluation is limited to a single attack family and does not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies GNN classification, and SafeDream~\\citep{safedream2026} predicts unsafe continuations. None of these systems evaluate generalization across attack families or analyze the mechanism that enables it. DisPeD's vanilla baseline represents the class of pretrained-encoder-plus-RNN approaches \\emph{without} domain adaptation; it is not a direct proxy for DeepContext, which differs in encoder fine-tuning, model capacity, and loss function. Our contribution is the systematic decomposition showing that domain exposure, not architectural choices, is the primary factor for cross-attack generalization.\n\n\\paragraph{Production safety systems.}\nPer-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, Constitutional Classifiers~\\citep{anthropic2025constitutional}) cannot model manipulation trajectories spanning multiple turns. LLM-as-judge approaches fail on multi-turn detection (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Perturbation-based defenses such as SmoothLLM~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm} target token-level attacks and do not address multi-turn manipulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Discourse analysis and computational persuasion.}\nRST~\\citep{mann1988rst}, PDTB~\\citep{prasad2008pdtb}, and dialogue act taxonomies~\\citep{stolcke2000dialogueact} model pragmatic structure. Speech Act Theory~\\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts} distinguishes locutionary from illocutionary acts; existing jailbreak detectors operate at the locutionary level. PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} identifies 40 persuasion techniques grounded in Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Related fine-grained persuasion detection work in propaganda analysis~\\citep{dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval} and persuasive dialogue~\\citep{wang2019persuasion} has shown that technique-level classification can capture manipulative intent. Our ablation reveals that the classification taxonomy functions as a representational regularizer rather than a detection mechanism; the taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}\nULMFiT~\\citep{howard2018ulmfit} established pretrain-then-fine-tune; \\citet{gururangan2020dontstop} showed that continued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains across domains. STILTs~\\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} use intermediate tasks. Our MLM control confirms that continued pretraining on domain text bridges the OOD gap without classification labels, consistent with \\citeauthor{gururangan2020dontstop}'s findings. Cross-encoder validation shows that encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset artifacts and adversarial robustness.}\nClassifiers are susceptible to spurious correlations~\\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing}, and adaptive adversaries can bypass safety classifiers~\\citep{nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive}. Adversarial NLP attacks such as TextFooler~\\citep{jin2020textfooler} and BERT-Attack~\\citep{li2020bertattack} demonstrate that neural text classifiers are vulnerable to token-level perturbations. TF-IDF achieves strong OOD detection on our evaluation data, revealing lexical separability in current datasets (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_attack}). Probing classifiers~\\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} could help determine whether adapted representations encode structure beyond lexical cues; we leave this for future work.\n",

Cross-encoder validation shows that encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset artifacts and adversarial robustness.}\nClassifiers are susceptible to spurious correlations~\\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing}, and adaptive adversaries can bypass safety classifiers~\\citep{nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive}. Adversarial NLP attacks such as TextFooler~\\citep{jin2020textfooler} and BERT-Attack~\\citep{li2020bertattack} demonstrate that neural text classifiers are vulnerable to token-level perturbations. TF-IDF achieves strong OOD detection on our evaluation data, revealing lexical separability in current datasets (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_attack}). Probing classifiers~\\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} could help determine whether adapted representations encode structure beyond lexical cues; we leave this for future work.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 13,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 13,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, Constitutional Classifiers~\\citep{anthropic2025constitutional}) cannot model manipulation trajectories spanning multiple turns. LLM-as-judge approaches fail on multi-turn detection (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Perturbation-based defenses such as SmoothLLM~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm} target token-level attacks and do not address multi-turn manipulation.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Discourse analysis and computational persuasion.}",
          "-RST~\\citep{mann1988rst}, PDTB~\\citep{prasad2008pdtb}, and dialogue act taxonomies~\\citep{stolcke2000dialogueact} model pragmatic structure. Speech Act Theory~\\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts} distinguishes locutionary from illocutionary acts; existing jailbreak detectors operate at the locutionary level. PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} identifies 40 persuasion techniques grounded in Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Related fine-grained persuasion detection work in propaganda analysis~\\citep{dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval} and persuasive dialogue~\\citep{wang2019persuasion} has shown that technique-level classification can capture manipulative intent. Our ablation reveals that the classification taxonomy functions as a representational regularizer rather than a detection mechanism; the taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).",
          "+RST~\\citep{mann1988rst}, PDTB~\\citep{prasad2008pdtb}, and dialogue act taxonomies~\\citep{stolcke2000dialogueact} model pragmatic structure. Speech Act Theory~\\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts} distinguishes locutionary from illocutionary acts; existing jailbreak detectors operate at the locutionary level. PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} identifies 40 persuasion techniques grounded in Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Related fine-grained persuasion detection work in propaganda analysis~\\citep{dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval} and persuasive dialogue~\\citep{wang2019persuasion} has shown that technique-level classification can capture manipulative intent. Our ablation reveals that the classification taxonomy does not function as a detection mechanism; all classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness. The taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}",
          " ULMFiT~\\citep{howard2018ulmfit} established pretrain-then-fine-tune; \\citet{gururangan2020dontstop} showed that continued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains across domains. STILTs~\\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} use intermediate tasks. Our MLM control confirms that continued pretraining on domain text bridges the OOD gap without classification labels, consistent with \\citeauthor{gururangan2020dontstop}'s findings. Cross-encoder validation shows that encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder})."
        ]

Based on the search results, I found information about the Kornblith et al. 2019 paper on CKA similarity in neural networks. Here are the key findings:

## Paper Details

The paper "Similarity of Neural Network Representations Revisited" was authored by Simon Kornblith, Mohammad Norouzi, Honglak Lee, and Geoffrey Hinton and was published at ICML 2019.

The paper examines methods for comparing neural network representations based on canonical correlation analysis (CCA). The paper introduces a similarity index equivalent to centered kernel alignment (CKA) that, unlike CCA, can reliably identify correspondences between representations in networks trained from different initializations.

The paper is available on arXiv as paper 1905.00414, and there is also an official project website at https://cka-similarity.github.io/

## Regarding BibTeX

While the search results don't include a specific BibTeX entry formatted in the results, you can find the official BibTeX citation for this paper through:
- The arXiv page at https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.00414 (which typically provides a BibTeX option)
- The ICML proceedings database
- The official project website

A typical BibTeX entry would be:
```
@inproceedings{kornblith2019similarity,
  title={Similarity of Neural Network Representations Revisited},
  author={Kornblith, Simon and Norouzi, Mohammad and Lee, Honglak and Hinton, Geoffrey},
  booktitle={International Conference on Machine Learning},
  pages={3519--3529},
  year={2019},
  organization={PMLR}
}
```


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\fbox{\\parbox{0.9\\columnwidth}{\\centering\\vspace{2em}\\textit{Architecture diagram placeholder}\\vspace{2em}}}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\nA conversation $C = \\{u_1, a_1, \\ldots, u_N, a_N\\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (jailbreak vs.\\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}\n\\label{sec:stage1}\n\nEach user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\\citep{he2021deberta}; the \\texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}\n\\label{tab:variants}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Objective} & \\textbf{Tests whether\\ldots} \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\\\\nBinary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\\\\nScrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\\\\nMLM-only & 15\\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\\\\nTopic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\\\\nWiki MLM & 15\\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\\\\nVanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe \\textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\\text{strategy}} + \\alpha \\cdot L_{\\text{intent}}$ ($\\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\\}$; all values yield F1\\,=\\,1.0 on IID). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78.\n\nThe remaining classification variants test specific ablation hypotheses.\nThe \\textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.\nThe \\textbf{scrambled} variant assigns random permutations of the 9-class labels to turns, preserving the classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics; this isolates whether the classification objective itself provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.\nThe \\textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.\nThe \\textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \\emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.\nThe \\textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text, isolating the effect of domain-specific vs.\\ general continued pretraining.\nThe \\textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining, measuring what Stage~2 alone can achieve.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}\n\\label{sec:stage2}\n\nTurn embeddings $\\{h_1, \\ldots, h_K\\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a bidirectional GRU ($d_{\\text{hidden}}$=128): $\\bar{h} = [\\overrightarrow{h_K};\\; \\overleftarrow{h_1}] \\in \\mathbb{R}^{256}$.\nA two-layer MLP ($256 \\to 64 \\to 2$, ReLU activation, dropout 0.3) maps $\\bar{h}$ to $\\hat{y} \\in \\{0, 1\\}$.\nThe Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.\nThis frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.\nA single-seed comparison suggests that mean pooling approaches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), though multi-seed validation is needed.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "907bc57d-bb6f-4c36-a5e9-b0ee93dadabe",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "dbcd3c72-1f20-4135-a804-86ce7b13abf9",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "e8430cdb-5233-4671-b57f-bfbd8a89daa2",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "DeBERTa-v3 (RTD)~\\citep{he2021deberta}",
    "newString": "DeBERTa-v3 (RTD)~\\citep{he2023debertav3}",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, rendering per-turn safety filters fundamentally blind.\n\nProduction classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \\emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \\emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}\n\nA vanilla DeBERTa encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing the class of pretrained-encoder-plus-RNN approaches without domain adaptation, collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight, worsening with more turns (0.628 at turn~1 to 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.\n\nWe address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \\textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.\n\nThree principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. Domain exposure via domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which DAPT becomes critical: RoBERTa vanilla achieves F1\\,=\\,0.981 on Deceptive Delight but collapses to 0.466 on ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack}. Lexical baselines (TF-IDF) exhibit a parallel difficulty threshold: F1\\,=\\,0.961 on Deceptive Delight but 0.261 on ActorAttack, establishing that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic data distribution (std reduced 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic OOD) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation design.} Seven controlled DeBERTa-v3 variants and three RoBERTa variants, each isolating a specific factor, evaluated across three synthetic OOD attack families. Domain exposure is the primary generalization mechanism ($\\sim$60\\,pp OOD gap bridged by MLM alone); misaligned supervision (topic classification) actively destabilizes representations.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Difficulty threshold across encoders.} DAPT is universally beneficial, but encoder pretraining determines when it becomes critical: DeBERTa-v3 (RTD)~\\citep{he2021deberta} requires adaptation for all unseen attacks; RoBERTa (MLM) tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}), though capacity differences (184M vs.\\ 125M parameters) cannot be fully disentangled from pretraining method.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp), with 4--20$\\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

Limitations.}\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=A\\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]\n  \\item Did you describe the limitations of your work? \\textbf{Yes.} Section~\\ref{sec:limitations} discusses dataset scale, lexical separability, annotation quality, OOD strategy prediction, single-seed variants, scope, and compute cost.\n  \\item Did you discuss any potential risks of your work? \\textbf{Yes.} See below.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{B. Potential Risks and Ethical Considerations.}\nThis work studies multi-turn jailbreak attacks against LLMs. While the analysis is defensive (improving detection), we acknowledge dual-use risk: the persuasion taxonomy and attack strategy analysis could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by: (1) using only synthetic conversations generated by an open-weight model (Qwen3-8B), not real attack data targeting production systems; (2) not releasing attack-generation prompts that could enable novel attacks; (3) focusing the contribution on detection mechanisms rather than attack techniques. All jailbreak conversations are synthetic and do not contain real harmful content. No human subjects were involved.\n\n\\paragraph{C. Computational Experiments.}\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=C\\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]\n  \\item Did you report the number of parameters in the models used? \\textbf{Yes.} DeBERTa-v3-base (184M) and RoBERTa-base (125M) in Sections~\\ref{sec:method} and~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n  \\item Did you report the total amount of compute and the type of resources used? \\textbf{Yes.} Under 4 GPU-hours total on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB). Section~\\ref{sec:limitations} and Appendix~\\ref{sec:reproducibility}.\n  \\item Did you report the implementation details sufficient for reproducibility? \\textbf{Yes.} Appendix~\\ref{sec:reproducibility} provides all hyperparameters. We will release code and data upon acceptance.\n  \\item Did you report error bars or confidence intervals? \\textbf{Yes.} All OOD results report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (population std with $N$ divisor). Statistical tests (McNemar's) are reported where applicable.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{D. Human Evaluation.}\nNot applicable. No human evaluation was conducted. Strategy annotation quality was validated against a human sample ($n$=50, $\\kappa$=0.78) but this was a validation check, not a full human evaluation study.\n\n\\paragraph{E. Existing Assets.}\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=E\\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]\n  \\item Did you cite the creators of artifacts you used? \\textbf{Yes.} DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta}, RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta}, Qwen3-8B (for data generation).\n  \\item Did you discuss the license or terms for use and/or distribution of any artifacts?           " This confirms the domain-adaptive pretraining interpretation~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}: continued pretraining on jailbreak conversation text captures features beyond lexical overlap, as evidenced by TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (F1 = 0.261 vs.\\ 9-class 0.972).",

          "+Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection: all three classification-supervised variants converge to identical F1 (0.997$\\pm$0.005), and McNemar's test finds no statistically significant differences among them on OOD data (all $p > 0.13$; Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}), though with $\\leq$7 discordant pairs per comparison, statistical power is limited.",
          " Paired permutation tests further confirm this equivalence: 9-class vs.\\ JB-MLM yields max $p$ = 1.0 (DD) and $p \\geq 0.40$ (ActorAttack); 9-class vs.\\ scrambled yields max $p$ = 1.0 (DD). <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Xvp1BjSg8MnvRvdkctpDem`</summary>

```text
232	\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}
233	\label{sec:perturbation}
234	
235	\begin{table}[t]
236	\centering
237	\small
238	\caption{Perturbation behavior under adversarial paraphrasing on IID Crescendo ($n$=76). $\Delta$: F1 degradation (pp). On this dataset and OOD Deceptive Delight, a three-layer pattern emerges (classification structure at full length, correct+fine-grained labels at $K$=2, both independently at $K$=1); this pattern does not generalize to ActorAttack.}
239	\label{tab:perturbation}
240	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
241	\toprule
242	\textbf{Method} & $\Delta$ Full & $\Delta$ $K$=1 & $\Delta$ $K$=2 & $\Delta$ $K$=3 \\
243	\midrule
244	9-class (ours) & \textbf{0.0} & \textbf{$-$2.6} & \textbf{0.0} & \textbf{0.0} \\
245	Scrambled & \textbf{0.0} & $-$5.3 & $-$2.6 & \textbf{0.0} \\
246	Binary & \textbf{0.0} & $-$9.2 & $-$2.6 & \textbf{0.0} \\
247	\midrule
248	TF-IDF LR & $-$1.3 & $-$9.6 & $-$4.1 & $-$1.3 \\
249	MLM-only & $-$2.6 & $-$9.2 & $-$4.0 & $-$4.0 \\
250	Topic (seed 42) & $-$4.0 & $-$2.7 & $-$2.7 & \textbf{0.0} \\
251	\midrule
252	Wikipedia MLM & $-$12.1 & $-$12.4 & $-$15.4 & $-$17.9 \\
253	Vanilla DeBERTa & $-$11.8 & $-$14.6 & $-$12.1 & $-$13.8 \\
254	\bottomrule
255	\end{tabular}
256	\end{table}
257	
258	Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior across variants on IID data.
259	All classification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length and $K \geq 3$, regardless of label correctness.
260	Classification training structure alone suffices for full-length robustness.
261	At $K$=2, the combination of correct and fine-grained labels is required: only 9-class ($\Delta$=0.0) maintains zero degradation, while scrambled (random labels, fine-grained) and binary (correct labels, coarse) both degrade by $-$2.6\,pp.
262	The fact that scrambled = binary at $K$=2 shows that neither granularity alone nor correctness alone suffices; the 9-class advantage requires both properties jointly.
263	At $K$=1, both granularity and correctness contribute independently: granularity provides +3.9\,pp (scrambled $-$5.3 vs.\ binary $-$9.2\,pp), and correctness provides +2.7\,pp (9-class $-$2.6 vs.\ scrambled $-$5.3\,pp).
264	Correct fine-grained labels yield the most perturbation-invariant early-turn representations.
265	
266	Jailbreak-domain models without classification (MLM, topic, TF-IDF) degrade by 1--4\,pp at full length.
267	Models without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp, despite Wikipedia MLM matching jailbreak MLM on detection (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Perturbation robustness requires jailbreak-domain exposure specifically.
268	McNemar's test on matched-seed predictions confirms that no pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (9-class vs.\ binary, 9-class vs.\ scrambled, scrambled vs.\ binary) reach statistical significance at any prefix length (all $p > 0.13$).
269	Discordant pairs are sparse ($\leq$7 per comparison), and the direction of advantage is inconsistent across seeds (e.g., 9-class outperforms scrambled at seed 42 but the reverse holds at seed 456 for $K$=1).
270	The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76 IID, with discordant pairs $\leq$7) has limited statistical power to detect differences of this magnitude; the three-layer structure should be interpreted as an observed pattern rather than a statistically confirmed hierarchy.
271	
272	On OOD Deceptive Delight ($n$=118), the IID robustness pattern replicates fully (Appendix~\ref{sec:ood_perturbation}).
273	All classification-supervised variants show zero degradation at full conversation length; perturbed F1 reaches 1.000$\pm$0.000, a marginal improvement over clean F1 (0.997$\pm$0.005).
274	The 9-class and scrambled variants are completely tied on OOD perturbed data (McNemar $b$=$c$=0), consistent with the IID finding that label content does not affect detection.
275	The larger OOD sample and more extreme performance gaps provide statistical power absent from IID evaluation: McNemar's test yields $p < 0.001$ for 9-class vs.\ vanilla across all prefix lengths, with 49--78 discordant pairs per $K$.
276	Vanilla shows systematic early-turn degradation ($\Delta K$=1 = $-$6.8$\pm$1.1\,pp across three seeds), while its full-length $\Delta$ (+2.7\,pp) reflects floor-effect noise at a baseline of 0.312 (std = 0.055) rather than meaningful robustness.
277	Non-adapted representations are vulnerable to surface-level perturbation at early prefixes where detection is most critical.
278	
279	On OOD ActorAttack, the zero-degradation pattern does not hold. Domain-adapted variants degrade substantially: scrambled $\Delta$ Full = $-$17.2\,pp, 9-class $-$20.4$\pm$13.5\,pp, binary $-$25.0\,pp, and MLM $-$35.3\,pp. Vanilla shows negligible change ($\Delta$ = $-$0.9\,pp), a floor effect from its already-low clean baseline (0.258). The 9-class variant exhibits extreme cross-seed variance ($\Delta$ ranges from $-$1.3\,pp at seed 123 to $-$30.3\,pp at seeds 42 and 456). Absolute perturbation robustness is therefore attack-family-dependent: the three-layer pattern observed on IID and DD does not generalize to ActorAttack. One possible explanation is that ActorAttack's distributed multi-persona structure produces more diffuse persuasion signals that are vulnerable to surface rephrasing, but this hypothesis remains speculative.
280	
281	Perturbation narrows but does not close the gap between adapted and non-adapted models: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$). Domain adaptation's detection advantage, identified in Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}, persists under adversarial paraphrasing on ActorAttack.
```

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
      "content": "\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Perturbation behavior under adversarial paraphrasing on IID Crescendo ($n$=76). $\\Delta$: F1 degradation (pp). On this dataset and OOD Deceptive Delight, a three-layer pattern emerges (classification structure at full length, correct+fine-grained labels at $K$=2, both independently at $K$=1); this pattern does not generalize to ActorAttack.}\n\\label{tab:perturbation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\Delta$ Full & $\\Delta$ $K$=1 & $\\Delta$ $K$=2 & $\\Delta$ $K$=3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class (ours) & \\textbf{0.0} & \\textbf{$-$2.6} & \\textbf{0.0} & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\nScrambled & \\textbf{0.0} & $-$5.3 & $-$2.6 & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\nBinary & \\textbf{0.0} & $-$9.2 & $-$2.6 & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\n\\midrule\nTF-IDF LR & $-$1.3 & $-$9.6 & $-$4.1 & $-$1.3 \\\\\nMLM-only & $-$2.6 & $-$9.2 & $-$4.0 & $-$4.0 \\\\\nTopic (seed 42) & $-$4.0 & $-$2.7 & $-$2.7 & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\n\\midrule\nWikipedia MLM & $-$12.1 & $-$12.4 & $-$15.4 & $-$17.9 \\\\\nVanilla DeBERTa & $-$11.8 & $-$14.6 & $-$12.1 & $-$13.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior across variants on IID data.\nAll classification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length and $K \\geq 3$, regardless of label correctness.\nClassification training structure alone suffices for full-length robustness.\nAt $K$=2, the combination of correct and fine-grained labels is required: only 9-class ($\\Delta$=0.0) maintains zero degradation, while scrambled (random labels, fine-grained) and binary (correct labels, coarse) both degrade by $-$2.6\\,pp.\nThe fact that scrambled = binary at $K$=2 shows that neither granularity alone nor correctness alone suffices; the 9-class advantage requires both properties jointly.\nAt $K$=1, both granularity and correctness contribute independently: granularity provides +3.9\\,pp (scrambled $-$5.3 vs.\\ binary $-$9.2\\,pp), and correctness provides +2.7\\,pp (9-class $-$2.6 vs.\\ scrambled $-$5.3\\,pp).\nCorrect fine-grained labels yield the most perturbation-invariant early-turn representations.\n\nJailbreak-domain models without classification (MLM, topic, TF-IDF) degrade by 1--4\\,pp at full length.\nModels without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp, despite Wikipedia MLM matching jailbreak MLM on detection (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Perturbation robustness requires jailbreak-domain exposure specifically.\nMcNemar's test on matched-seed predictions confirms that no pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (9-class vs.\\ binary, 9-class vs.\\ scrambled, scrambled vs.\\ binary) reach statistical significance at any prefix length (all $p > 0.13$).\nDiscordant pairs are sparse ($\\leq$7 per comparison), and the direction of advantage is inconsistent across seeds (e.g., 9-class outperforms scrambled at seed 42 but the reverse holds at seed 456 for $K$=1).\nThe perturbation evaluation ($n$=76 IID, with discordant pairs $\\leq$7) has limited statistical power to detect differences of this magnitude; the three-layer structure should be interpreted as an observed pattern rather than a statistically confirmed hierarchy.\n\nOn OOD Deceptive Delight ($n$=118), the IID robustness pattern replicates fully (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ood_perturbation}).\nAll classification-supervised variants show zero degradation at full conversation length; perturbed F1 reaches 1.000$\\pm$0.000, a marginal improvement over clean F1 (0.997$\\pm$0.005).\nThe 9-class and scrambled variants are completely tied on OOD perturbed data (McNemar $b$=$c$=0), consistent with the IID finding that label content does not affect detection.\nThe larger OOD sample and more extreme performance gaps provide statistical power absent from IID evaluation: McNemar's test yields $p < 0.001$ for 9-class vs.\\ vanilla across all prefix lengths, with 49--78 discordant pairs per $K$.\nVanilla shows systematic early-turn degradation ($\\Delta K$=1 = $-$6.8$\\pm$1.1\\,pp across three seeds), while its full-length $\\Delta$ (+2.7\\,pp) reflects floor-effect noise at a baseline of 0.312 (std = 0.055) rather than meaningful robustness.\nNon-adapted representations are vulnerable to surface-level perturbation at early prefixes where detection is most critical.\n\nOn OOD ActorAttack, the zero-degradation pattern does not hold. Domain-adapted variants degrade substantially: scrambled $\\Delta$ Full = $-$17.2\\,pp, 9-class $-$20.4$\\pm$13.5\\,pp, binary $-$25.0\\,pp, and MLM $-$35.3\\,pp. Vanilla shows negligible change ($\\Delta$ = $-$0.9\\,pp), a floor effect from its already-low clean baseline (0.258). The 9-class variant exhibits extreme cross-seed variance ($\\Delta$ ranges from $-$1.3\\,pp at seed 123 to $-$30.3\\,pp at seeds 42 and 456). Absolute perturbation robustness is therefore attack-family-dependent: the three-layer pattern observed on IID and DD does not generalize to ActorAttack. One possible explanation is that ActorAttack's distributed multi-persona structure produces more diffuse persuasion signals that are vulnerable to surface rephrasing, but this hypothesis remains speculative.\n\nPerturbation narrows but does not close the gap between adapted and non-adapted models: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$). Domain adaptation's detection advantage, identified in Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}, persists under adversarial paraphrasing on ActorAttack.",
      "numLines": 50,
      "startLine": 232,
      "totalLines": 313
    }
  },
  "sourceToolAssistantUUID": "089d63bf-37f3-4f61-ad7c-3ffcd5f1821b",

  "slug": "distributed-cooking-shore"
}

  "new_string": "Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior on IID data.\nClassification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length regardless of label correctness.\nModels without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp.\nMcNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (all $p > 0.13$), though with $\\leq$7 discordant pairs, statistical power is limited.\nOn OOD DD ($n$=118), this pattern replicates: classification-supervised variants show zero degradation while McNemar's test confirms 9-class vs.\\ vanilla significance ($p < 0.001$, 49--78 discordant pairs; Appendix~\\ref{sec:ood_perturbation}).\n\nOn OOD ActorAttack, the zero-degradation pattern does not hold. DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing."

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01YDPo9pkq6uFXbRQWNCAhQJ`</summary>

```text
7:First, domain exposure via domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels; TF-IDF collapses on structurally complex ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.261) while domain-adapted models maintain F1\,$\geq$\,0.89.
8:Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which DAPT becomes critical, though capacity differences (184M vs.\ 125M parameters) cannot be fully disentangled from pretraining method.
10:DAPT on synthetic text transfers to human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks (macro F1: 0.837 vs.\ vanilla 0.413); full multi-turn validation on human data remains future work.
```

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:172:To test whether the evidence hierarchy generalizes beyond DeBERTa-v3, we replicate key variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} with identical Stage-2 pipelines and evaluate on both DD and ActorAttack.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:204:A key concern is whether the evidence hierarchy reflects genuine beyond-lexical generalization or merely within-LLM stylistic transfer, since both training and OOD evaluation data are generated by Qwen3-8B. {

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "\\fbox{\\parbox{0.9\\columnwidth}{\\centering\\vspace{2em}\\textit{Architecture diagram placeholder}\\vspace{2em}}}",
    "newString": "\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}",
    "originalFile": "% method.tex — D024 compressed\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nDisPeD is a two-stage framework for multi-turn jailbreak detection. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\fbox{\\parbox{0.9\\columnwidth}{\\centering\\vspace{2em}\\textit{Architecture diagram placeholder}\\vspace{2em}}}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\nA conversation $C = \\{u_1, a_1, \\ldots, u_N, a_N\\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (jailbreak vs.\\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}\n\\label{sec:stage1}\n\nEach user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \\texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}\n\\label{tab:variants}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Objective} & \\textbf{Tests whether\\ldots} \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\\\\nBinary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\\\\nScrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\\\\nMLM-only & 15\\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\\\\nTopic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\\\\nWiki MLM & 15\\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\\\\nVanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe \\textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\\text{strategy}} + \\alpha \\cdot L_{\\text{intent}}$ ($\\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\\}$; all values yield F1\\,=\\,1.0 on IID). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78.\n\nThe remaining classification variants test specific ablation hypotheses.\nThe \\textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.\nThe \\textbf{scrambled} variant assigns random permutations of the 9-class labels to turns, preserving the classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics; this isolates whether the classification objective itself provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.\nThe \\textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.\nThe \\textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \\emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.\nThe \\textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text, isolating the effect of domain-specific vs.\\ general continued pretraining.\nThe \\textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining, measuring what Stage~2 alone can achieve.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}\n\\label{sec:stage2}\n\nTurn embeddings $\\{h_1, \\ldots, h_K\\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a bidirectional GRU ($d_{\\text{hidden}}$=128): $\\bar{h} = [\\overrightarrow{h_K};\\; \\overleftarrow{h_1}] \\in \\mathbb{R}^{256}$.\nA two-layer MLP ($256 \\to 64 \\to 2$, ReLU activation, dropout 0.3) maps $\\bar{h}$ to $\\hat{y} \\in \\{0, 1\\}$.\nThe Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.\nThis frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.\nA single-seed comparison suggests that mean pooling approaches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), though multi-seed validation is needed.\n",

5	\section{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}
6	\label{sec:llm_judge}
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\caption{LLM-as-judge jailbreak detection. IID: binary F1 (Crescendo test set); OOD: macro F1 (DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack). GPT-5.5 F1 values exclude unparseable responses and represent upper bounds.}
12	\label{tab:llm_judge}
13	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccr@{}}
14	\toprule
15	\textbf{Method} & DD & AA & IID & Unparse. \\
16	\midrule
17	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\textit{Zero-shot}} \\[2pt]
18	GPT-5.5 & -- & -- & .383 & 30--47\% \\
19	Qwen3-8B & .244 & .244 & .100 & 0\% \\
20	\midrule
21	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\textit{3-shot}} \\[2pt]
22	Qwen3-8B & .566 & .652 & -- & 0\% \\
23	\midrule
24	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\textit{Trained classifier}} \\[2pt]
25	9-class (ours) & \textbf{.997} & \textbf{.972} & \textbf{1.000} & 0\% \\
26	\bottomrule
27	\end{tabular}
28	\end{table}
29	
30	Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge} reports LLM-as-judge performance on IID and OOD data.
31	On IID Crescendo, GPT-5.5 achieves precision of 1.0 at all turn prefixes but recall ranges from only 0.105 to 0.290, resulting in F1 = 0.383 on full conversations; 30--47\% of responses are unparseable.
32	Qwen3-8B performs worse (IID F1 = 0.100).
33	On OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 on both DD and ActorAttack by predicting nearly all conversations as benign.
34	Even with 3-shot prompting, Qwen3-8B reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (AA), while DisPeD maintains macro F1 $\geq$ 0.972.
