_source line 1176 · 2026-05-18T19:28:53.493Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user (Director) issued a series of sequential patch instructions to the Paper Agent:
   
   a. **exp9 Bootstrap CI completion** (carried over): Add RoBERTa vs DeBERTa vanilla CI to §4.4, then verify and report.
   
   b. **Compilation fix**: Fix 5 undefined citations + missing fig1_architecture.pdf to achieve 0 errors before Round 3 submission.
   
   c. **Comprehensive Revision Round 3 (16 items, DDL 2026-05-25)**: P0 (MF5 data split transparency), P1 (MF2 cross-attack scope limitation, MF3 Finding 3 restructure removing "regularizer", MF4-A discourse-level→beyond-lexical), P2 (SF3 ActorAttack narrative, SF6 Abstract compression, SF7 DeepContext unification, SF9 citation additions), P3 (SF2 McNemar caveat, SF4 encoder capacity caveat, SF8 Figure 3 caption, SF10 perturbation compression, SF11 terminology, M1-M7 minor batch).
   
   d. **Figure integration + FITD fix + font check (latest)**: (1) Replace fig1 placeholder with actual PDF, (2) Add CKA heatmap to paper, (3) Update FITD macro F1 if referenced.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **DAPT (domain-adaptive continued pretraining)**: Primary mechanism for cross-attack generalization
   - **Evidence hierarchy**: Ordering of encoder variants by OOD detection performance
   - **Beyond-lexical features**: Replaces "discourse-level" throughout paper; evidenced by TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261) vs domain-adapted models (≥0.89)
   - **Distribution-specific classification effects**: New Finding 3 framing — classification supervision stabilizes within synthetic distribution but does NOT transfer to human data (reversal on ToxicChat)
   - **BCa Bootstrap CI + paired permutation tests**: Statistical rigor additions for R1-N3-v2
   - **Data split transparency**: IID 500 = train(348)+val(76)+test(76, 38JB+38B); OOD = 80JB + 38 shared IID-test benign = 118 total per family
   - **Centered CKA**: Representation similarity measure, now cited as kornblith2019cka
   - **Macro F1**: Standard metric for all human data (ToxicChat) and LLM-judge OOD results
   - **"Regularizer" removal**: Director mandated removing this term — only 2 distribution points + ToxicChat single-turn insufficient to support mechanistic claim

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`docs/paper/experiments.tex`** (~310 lines, most heavily edited)
     - P0 MF5: Data paragraph rewritten with full split transparency (lines 9-19)
     - MF4-A: "discourse-level generalization" → "beyond-lexical generalization" (line 201)
     - MF4-A: Removed speculative feature list, replaced with TF-IDF evidence (line 107 area)
     - MF3: Multiple "regularizer" removals in §4.3, §4.4, §4.5, §4.6, §4.7

       \begin{abstract}
       Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across seemingly benign conversational turns, evading per-turn safety filters.
       ...Three findings emerge.
       First, domain exposure via domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels; TF-IDF collapses on structurally complex ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.261) while domain-adapted models maintain F1\,$\geq$\,0.89.
       Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which DAPT becomes critical, though capacity differences (184M vs.\ 125M parameters) cannot be fully disentangled from pretraining method.
       Third, classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic training distribution (variance reduced 4--20$\times$ on synthetic OOD) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.
       DAPT on synthetic text transfers to human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks (macro F1: 0.837 vs.\ vanilla 0.413); full multi-turn validation on human data remains future work.
       \end{abstract}
       ```

        "content": "% method.tex — D024 compressed\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nDisPeD is a two-stage framework for multi-turn jailbreak detection. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\nA conversation $C = \\{u_1, a_1, \\ldots, u_N, a_N\\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (jailbreak vs.\\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}\n\\label{sec:stage1}\n\nEach user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \\texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}\n\\label{tab:variants}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Objective} & \\textbf{Tests whether\\ldots} \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\\\\nBinary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\\\\nScrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\\\\nMLM-only & 15\\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\\\\nTopic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\\\\nWiki MLM & 15\\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\\\\nVanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe \\textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\\text{strategy}} + \\alpha \\cdot L_{\\text{intent}}$ ($\\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\\}$; all values yield F1\\,=\\,1.0 on IID). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78.\n\nThe remaining classification variants test specific ablation hypotheses.\nThe \\textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.\nThe \\textbf{scrambled} variant assigns random permutations of the 9-class labels to turns, preserving the classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics; this isolates whether the classification objective itself provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.\nThe \\textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.\nThe \\textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \\emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.\nThe \\textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text, isolating the effect of domain-specific vs.\\ general continued pretraining.\nThe \\textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining, measuring what Stage~2 alone can achieve.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}\n\\label{sec:stage2}\n\nTurn embeddings $\\{h_1, \\ldots, h_K\\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a bidirectional GRU ($d_{\\text{hidden}}$=128): $\\bar{h} = [\\overrightarrow{h_K};\\; \\overleftarrow{h_1}] \\in \\mathbb{R}^{256}$.\nA two-layer MLP ($256 \\to 64 \\to 2$, ReLU activation, dropout 0.3) maps $\\bar{h}$ to $\\hat{y} \\in \\{0, 1\\}$.\nThe Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.\nThis frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.\nA single-seed comparison suggests that mean pooling approaches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), though multi-seed validation is needed.\n",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 62
      }

Potential Risks and Ethical Considerations.}\nThis work studies multi-turn jailbreak attacks against LLMs. While the analysis is defensive (improving detection), we acknowledge dual-use risk: the persuasion taxonomy and attack strategy analysis could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by: (1) using only synthetic conversations generated by an open-weight model (Qwen3-8B), not real attack data targeting production systems; (2) not releasing attack-generation prompts that could enable novel attacks; (3) focusing the contribution on detection mechanisms rather than attack techniques. All jailbreak conversations are synthetic and do not contain real harmful content. No human subjects were involved.\n\n\\paragraph{C. Computational Experiments.}\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=C\\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]\n  \\item Did you report the number of parameters in the models used? \\textbf{Yes.} DeBERTa-v3-base (184M) and RoBERTa-base (125M) in Sections~\\ref{sec:method} and~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n  \\item Did you report the total amount of compute and the type of resources used? \\textbf{Yes.} Under 4 GPU-hours total on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB). Section~\\ref{sec:limitations} and Appendix~\\ref{sec:reproducibility}.\n  \\item Did you report the implementation details sufficient for reproducibility? \\textbf{Yes.} Appendix~\\ref{sec:reproducibility} provides all hyperparameters. We will release code and data upon acceptance.\n  \\item Did you report error bars or confidence intervals? \\textbf{Yes.} All OOD results report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (sample standard deviation with Bessel's correction, $N{-}1$ divisor). Statistical tests (McNemar's, bootstrap CIs, permutation tests) are reported where applicable.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{D. Human Evaluation.}\nNot applicable. No human evaluation was conducted. Strategy annotation quality was validated against a human sample ($n$=50, $\\kappa$=0.78) but this was a validation check, not a full human evaluation study.\n\n\\paragraph{E. Code will be released under MIT. DeBERTa-v3 is available under MIT; RoBERTa under MIT; Qwen3-8B under Apache 2.0.\n  \\item Did you discuss whether the data you are using contains personally identifiable information? \\textbf{N/A.} All data is synthetically generated. ```json
{
  "parentUuid": "997ec5ae-43bd-4397-8619-51491e19a2b8",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, rendering per-turn safety filters fundamentally blind.\n\nProduction classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \\emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \\emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}\n\nA vanilla DeBERTa encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation, representing the class of pretrained-encoder-plus-RNN approaches without domain adaptation, collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.312$\\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight, worsening with more turns (0.628 at turn~1 to 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.\n\nWe address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \\textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.\n\nThree principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on synthetic multi-turn data spanning four attack families. Domain exposure via domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which DAPT becomes critical: RoBERTa vanilla achieves F1\\,=\\,0.981 on Deceptive Delight but collapses to 0.466 on ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack}. Lexical baselines (TF-IDF) exhibit a parallel difficulty threshold: F1\\,=\\,0.961 on Deceptive Delight but 0.261 on ActorAttack, establishing that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words. Classification supervision stabilizes predictions within the synthetic data distribution (std reduced 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic OOD) regardless of label correctness, but this benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where all classification variants underperform pure MLM.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation design.} Seven controlled DeBERTa-v3 variants and three RoBERTa variants, each isolating a specific factor, evaluated across three synthetic OOD attack families. Domain exposure is the primary generalization mechanism ($\\sim$60\\,pp OOD gap bridged by MLM alone); misaligned supervision (topic classification) actively destabilizes representations.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Difficulty threshold across encoders.} DAPT is universally beneficial, but encoder pretraining determines when it becomes critical: DeBERTa-v3 (RTD)~\\citep{he2023debertav3} requires adaptation for all unseen attacks; RoBERTa (MLM) tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}), though capacity differences (184M vs.\\ 125M parameters) cannot be fully disentangled from pretraining method.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Distribution-specific classification effects.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp), with 4--20$\\times$ cross-seed variance reduction on synthetic OOD evaluations vs.\\ MLM-only. This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 24,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 24
      }
    },

```text
1	% experiments.tex — D024 compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	\label{sec:experiments}
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Data.}
10	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
11	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (348), validation (76), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
12	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
13	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
14	No overlap exists in attack templates across families.
15	The OOD benign set is IID (same generation pipeline as training); the OOD challenge is in distinguishing novel attack families from familiar benign conversations.
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
22	We also compare TF-IDF LR and LLM-as-judge baselines (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}): GPT-5.5 achieves only F1 = 0.191--0.449 on IID Crescendo with 30--47\% unparseable responses; on OOD data, zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves macro F1 = 0.244 (DD and ActorAttack) by predicting nearly all conversations as benign, and even 3-shot prompting reaches only 0.566 (DD) and 0.652 (ActorAttack). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.
23	
24	\paragraph{Metrics.}
25	F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012WhhVGoSATmyYseNJwv1Xx`</summary>

```text
24	\label{sec:limitations}
25	
26	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
27	The training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and all multi-turn OOD evaluation data is also Qwen3-8B-generated. This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. While ToxicChat (human-authored, single-turn) confirms that DAPT's detection advantage transfers across data sources (+18--42\,pp over vanilla; Section~\ref{sec:human_data}), multi-turn generalization across LLM generation sources remains to be verified. Whether the full evidence hierarchy, including classification supervision's within-distribution stabilization, generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks is open.
28	
29	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
30	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures beyond-lexical features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this beyond-lexical advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
31	
```

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:191:Centered CKA~\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms that both continued pretraining and classification supervision substantially reshape the representation space on both encoders.

### Tool result

_source line 1244 · 2026-05-18T19:45:56.532Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XQXEVYG8GrCjkwy5ENd5Eq`</summary>

```text
185	
186	On synthetic OOD data, classification supervision provides a consistent stability benefit across both encoders and attack types: cross-seed variance drops to 0.005--0.046 (9-class) from 0.026--0.201 (vanilla/MLM).
187	RoBERTa's IID strategy prediction accuracy (0.84) is lower than DeBERTa's (1.0), yet detection F1 is comparable; this further supports the interpretation that classification supervision does not improve detection accuracy over domain exposure alone (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).
188	Perturbation robustness on RoBERTa is left for future work; the analysis in Section~\ref{sec:perturbation} is DeBERTa-specific.
189	
190	\paragraph{Representational analysis.}
191	Centered CKA~\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms that both continued pretraining and classification supervision substantially reshape the representation space on both encoders.
192	On IID data, vanilla-to-MLM CKA is 0.442 (DeBERTa) and 0.393 (RoBERTa); vanilla-to-9-class CKA is 0.393 and 0.592, respectively.
193	Both encoders undergo large representational shifts, yet these shifts yield strikingly different detection gains: DeBERTa MLM gains $\sim$60\,pp while RoBERTa MLM gains only 0.6\,pp from comparable CKA shifts.
194	The difference is explained by the starting point.
195	DeBERTa's RTD-pretrained representations begin far from the detection-optimal subspace (vanilla F1 = 0.312 on DD), so any reshaping yields large returns; RoBERTa's MLM-pretrained representations lie closer to this subspace on lexically separable attacks (vanilla F1 = 0.981 on DD) but collapse on structurally complex ones (0.466 on ActorAttack), where the reshaping benefit becomes substantial.
196	MLM and 9-class create distinct representation geometries (OOD CKA between them is 0.183 on RoBERTa), consistent with the distinct effects of unsupervised exposure and classification supervision identified in Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}.
197	Linear probing on three diagnostic tasks (turn position, escalation phase, persuasion presence) shows a consistent pattern across both encoders: 9-class outperforms vanilla and MLM by 1--5\,pp, while MLM and vanilla perform comparably.
198	Classification supervision enriches task-relevant features; unsupervised MLM reshapes representations without improving diagnostic separability.
199	
200	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
201	\subsection{Human Data Validation}
202	\label{sec:human_data}
203	
204	A key concern is whether the evidence hierarchy reflects genuine beyond-lexical generalization or merely within-LLM stylistic transfer, since both training and OOD evaluation data are generated by Qwen3-8B. We test this by evaluating on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat}, a dataset of 204 human-written jailbreak attempts and 204 benign conversations collected from real LMSYS user interactions, entirely independent of any LLM generation pipeline.
205	
206	\begin{table}[t]
207	\centering
208	\small
209	\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1). 3-seed mean$\pm$std. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}
```
